Uh… yeah, they sent me over from the station. I’m an EMT, and I guess things got… tense in the bay. I was moving the airway bags around, putting them into the pattern that was right—like, it had to line up with the dispatch numbers, so I kept saying the numbers out loud to keep track.

My lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t just step away. If I broke the sequence, it felt like the whole bay would go bad, like everything would be off. Like when the gear isn’t checked right and then a call drops and everybody’s scrambling—back in training they used to mark us down for stupid little placement things, but this wasn’t stupid. It felt important. I couldn’t just leave it half-done.\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought something bad would happen if they weren't, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… not like I saw a specific person getting hurt right then, but it felt like if I didn’t finish the pattern, if the airway bags weren’t lined up with the dispatch numbers, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI kept saying the numbers out loud, because that’s how I was keeping it straight, and my lieutenant was telling me to step away, but I couldn’t. It was like breaking the sequence would mess everything up. And airway bags are important anyway—you miss one thing in those and then everybody acts surprised later, you know?\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought it would prevent something dirty or contaminated from happening?\n\nPatient: Dirty or contaminated? I don’t… I don’t think that was the main thing. Yeah… I mean, I guess I did feel like it would be on me somehow. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.

My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.

I’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.

My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.

Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.

Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.

That’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.

It’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.

At the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.

And the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.

And I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.

My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.

My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.

Eventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.

And friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.

I was moving the airway bags around, putting them into the pattern that was right—like, it had to line up with the dispatch numbers, so I kept saying the numbers out loud to keep track.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t just step away. If I broke the sequence, it felt like the whole bay would go bad, like everything would be off. And the airway bags, you know, people don’t even check them the way they should sometimes—back when I was in school we had this instructor who was real strict about placement—anyway, they thought I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to put the airway bags in that specific pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it wasn’t just me being picky. It felt like they had to go that way, the right pattern, with the dispatch numbers. I kept saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nAnd my lieutenant kept saying, “Step away,” but if I stopped in the middle, it was like… the whole bay would be wrong, you know? Like when the gear isn’t checked right and then a call drops and everybody’s scrambling—back in training they used to mark us down for stupid little placement things, but this wasn’t stupid. It felt important. I couldn’t just leave it half-done.\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought something bad would happen if they weren't, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… not like I saw a specific person getting hurt right then, but it felt like if I didn’t finish the pattern, if the airway bags weren’t lined up with the dispatch numbers, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI kept saying the numbers out loud, because that’s how I was keeping it straight, and my lieutenant was telling me to step away, but I couldn’t. It was like breaking the sequence would mess everything up. And airway bags are important anyway—you miss one thing in those and then everybody acts surprised later, you know?\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought it would prevent something dirty or contaminated from happening?\n\nPatient: Dirty or contaminated? I don’t… I don’t think that was the main thing. I mean, airway bags should be clean, obviously, because you’re putting stuff near someone’s face and all that, and we had this one supervisor who used to go off about seals and packaging and—sorry.\n\nBut no, it wasn’t like I was thinking “contamination” exactly. It was more the pattern being wrong, the numbers not lining up right. Like if it wasn’t in the right order, the whole bay felt off.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you would be responsible if something went wrong later because the airway bags weren't in the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I did feel like it would be on me somehow. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.\n\nThat’s why I kept moving the airway bags back, lining them up with the dispatch numbers and saying the numbers out loud. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. At the station, when things are out of place, people act like it’s no big deal until a call comes in and then everyone’s yelling about where stuff is. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about putting things in the right pattern or order even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.\n\nI’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.

But “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.\n\nThat’s why I kept moving the airway bags back, lining them up with the dispatch numbers and saying the numbers out loud. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. At the station, when things are out of place, people act like it’s no big deal until a call comes in and then everyone’s yelling about where stuff is. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about putting things in the right pattern or order even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.\n\nI’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.

But it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.

So… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.

Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t want them, no. I mean… maybe before, at work, being organized was a good thing. On the rig you need things where they belong, because if you’re reaching for an airway bag you don’t want to be guessing, you know?

But this isn’t that. They sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over. My lieutenant told me to stop, told me to step away, and I just… couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.

So yeah, I wish I could stop. I don’t want to be like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.2, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh… yeah, they sent me over from the station. I’m an EMT, and I guess things got… tense in the bay. I was moving the airway bags around, putting them into the pattern that was right—like, it had to line up with the dispatch numbers, so I kept saying the numbers out loud to keep track.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t just step away. If I broke the sequence, it felt like the whole bay would go bad, like everything would be off. And the airway bags, you know, people don’t even check them the way they should sometimes—back when I was in school we had this instructor who was real strict about placement—anyway, they thought I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to put the airway bags in that specific pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it wasn’t just me being picky. It felt like they had to go that way, the right pattern, with the dispatch numbers. I kept saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nAnd my lieutenant kept saying, “Step away,” but if I stopped in the middle, it was like… the whole bay would be wrong, you know? Like when the gear isn’t checked right and then a call drops and everybody’s scrambling—back in training they used to mark us down for stupid little placement things, but this wasn’t stupid. It felt important. I couldn’t just leave it half-done.\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought something bad would happen if they weren't, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… not like I saw a specific person getting hurt right then, but it felt like if I didn’t finish the pattern, if the airway bags weren’t lined up with the dispatch numbers, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI kept saying the numbers out loud, because that’s how I was keeping it straight, and my lieutenant was telling me to step away, but I couldn’t. It was like breaking the sequence would mess everything up. And airway bags are important anyway—you miss one thing in those and then everybody acts surprised later, you know?\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought it would prevent something dirty or contaminated from happening?\n\nPatient: Dirty or contaminated? I don’t… I don’t think that was the main thing. I mean, airway bags should be clean, obviously, because you’re putting stuff near someone’s face and all that, and we had this one supervisor who used to go off about seals and packaging and—sorry.\n\nBut no, it wasn’t like I was thinking “contamination” exactly. It was more the pattern being wrong, the numbers not lining up right. Like if it wasn’t in the right order, the whole bay felt off.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you would be responsible if something went wrong later because the airway bags weren't in the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I did feel like it would be on me somehow. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.\n\nThat’s why I kept moving the airway bags back, lining them up with the dispatch numbers and saying the numbers out loud. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. At the station, when things are out of place, people act like it’s no big deal until a call comes in and then everyone’s yelling about where stuff is. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about putting things in the right pattern or order even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.\n\nI’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nBut it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you want to have, or do you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t want them, no. I mean… maybe before, at work, being organized was a good thing. On the rig you need things where they belong, because if you’re reaching for an airway bag you don’t want to be guessing, you know?\n\nBut this isn’t that. They sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over. My lieutenant told me to stop, told me to step away, and I just… couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo yeah, I wish I could stop. I don’t want to be like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I dislike it. I don’t want it there.

It’s not like I’m proud of what happened at the station. I kept moving the airway bags around into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, and my lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away. I heard him, I did, but it was like if I stopped in the middle of it the whole bay would go bad somehow.

And that sounds stupid when I say it here, because airway bags are just airway bags, but at the time it didn’t feel small. It felt like the sequence mattered more than anything else. So yes, I wish I could control it. I wish I could just drop it when someone tells me to.

Like when the gear isn’t checked right and then a call drops and everybody’s scrambling—back in training they used to mark us down for stupid little placement things, but this wasn’t stupid. It felt important. I couldn’t just leave it half-done.\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought something bad would happen if they weren't, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… not like I saw a specific person getting hurt right then, but it felt like if I didn’t finish the pattern, if the airway bags weren’t lined up with the dispatch numbers, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI kept saying the numbers out loud, because that’s how I was keeping it straight, and my lieutenant was telling me to step away, but I couldn’t. It was like breaking the sequence would mess everything up. And airway bags are important anyway—you miss one thing in those and then everybody acts surprised later, you know?\n\nDoctor: Was your concern about the airway bags being in the right pattern because you thought it would prevent something dirty or contaminated from happening?\n\nPatient: Dirty or contaminated? I don’t… I don’t think that was the main thing. I mean, airway bags should be clean, obviously, because you’re putting stuff near someone’s face and all that, and we had this one supervisor who used to go off about seals and packaging and—sorry.\n\nBut no, it wasn’t like I was thinking “contamination” exactly. It was more the pattern being wrong, the numbers not lining up right. Like if it wasn’t in the right order, the whole bay felt off.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you would be responsible if something went wrong later because the airway bags weren't in the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I did feel like it would be on me somehow. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.\n\nThat’s why I kept moving the airway bags back, lining them up with the dispatch numbers and saying the numbers out loud. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. At the station, when things are out of place, people act like it’s no big deal until a call comes in and then everyone’s yelling about where stuff is. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about putting things in the right pattern or order even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.\n\nI’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. Yeah… I guess it has. I don’t really see people much now.

It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nBut it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you want to have, or do you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t want them, no. I mean… maybe before, at work, being organized was a good thing. On the rig you need things where they belong, because if you’re reaching for an airway bag you don’t want to be guessing, you know?\n\nBut this isn’t that. They sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over. My lieutenant told me to stop, told me to step away, and I just… couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo yeah, I wish I could stop. I don’t want to be like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you dislike having and wish you could control or eliminate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I dislike it. I don’t want it there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m proud of what happened at the station. I kept moving the airway bags around into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, and my lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away. I heard him, I did, but it was like if I stopped in the middle of it the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd that sounds stupid when I say it here, because airway bags are just airway bags, but at the time it didn’t feel small. It felt like the sequence mattered more than anything else. So yes, I wish I could control it. I wish I could just drop it when someone tells me to.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and behaviors about patterns and sequences made it difficult for you to interact with friends or family, or to enjoy activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. I don’t really see people much now.\n\nLike the guys used to call me for basketball, and I’d say I was coming, or at least I’d think about it. Now most days I just sit in my apartment. There’s the trays in the corner, food still sitting in the fridge, and I keep thinking I should cook something, because I used to like cooking, you know, doing it right, getting everything lined up on the counter—\n\nBut then I just don’t. I don’t call anybody back. I don’t start anything. It’s not even always the patterns right in that second… it’s like I can’t get myself to care enough to move. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it doesn’t feel like me. I mean, I was always the guy who kept the rig straight, kept things where they belonged. That made sense. You check your airway bag, you know where the laryngoscope is, the mask, the tubing—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked exactly.

But what happened at the station… they sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop and step away, and I just wouldn’t. I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.

That’s not who I thought I was. I’m supposed to be useful there, not… standing in the bay doing that while people are telling me to stop.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh… yeah, they sent me over from the station. I’m an EMT, and I guess things got… tense in the bay. I was moving the airway bags around, putting them into the pattern that was right—like, it had to line up with the dispatch numbers, so I kept saying the numbers out loud to keep track.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t just step away. If I broke the sequence, it felt like the whole bay would go bad, like everything would be off. And the airway bags, you know, people don’t even check them the way they should sometimes—back when I was in school we had this instructor who was real strict about placement—anyway, they thought I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to put the airway bags in that specific pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it wasn’t just me being picky. It felt like they had to go that way, the right pattern, with the dispatch numbers. I kept saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nAnd my lieutenant kept saying, “Step away,” but if I stopped in the middle, it was like… the whole bay would be wrong, you know? It was more the pattern being wrong, the numbers not lining up right. Like if it wasn’t in the right order, the whole bay felt off.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you would be responsible if something went wrong later because the airway bags weren't in the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I did feel like it would be on me somehow. Not in a clean way, like I could explain it in a report, but if I walked away when the pattern wasn’t right, then later something went wrong in the bay, it would be because I broke it.\n\nThat’s why I kept moving the airway bags back, lining them up with the dispatch numbers and saying the numbers out loud. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, step away, but I couldn’t just leave it like that. So, yeah… it felt like if the sequence got broken, the whole bay would go bad, and I’d be the one who let it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about putting things in the right pattern or order even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not just work, but the station is where it got noticed because everyone could see me doing it.\n\nI’ll start thinking things need to line up a certain way, like there’s a right sequence, and if I leave it wrong then everything feels… bad, like it’s going to throw the whole place off. That’s what happened with the airway bags. I kept moving them into the pattern that made sense to me, with the dispatch numbers, saying the numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, and I heard him, I did, but I couldn’t just break the sequence. It felt like if I did, the whole bay would go bad. Not like—sorry, I know you asked not at work—but that’s the clearest example. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nBut it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you want to have, or do you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t want them, no. I mean… maybe before, at work, being organized was a good thing. On the rig you need things where they belong, because if you’re reaching for an airway bag you don’t want to be guessing, you know?\n\nBut this isn’t that. They sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over. I don’t want it there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m proud of what happened at the station. At home it’s more like I’ll keep noticing if something’s not in the right order and it sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I can be trying to do something else and then the sequence is just… there, like I have to check it or hold it in my head.\n\nThat’s kind of why they sent me here, I think. At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nBut it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you want to have, or do you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t want them, no. It doesn’t feel like I’m in control of it, no. I mean… I’m moving my hands, so I know it’s me doing it, but it feels like something’s pushing me along once the sequence starts.

Like at the station, with the airway bags—I kept putting them into this pattern because it just felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I heard him, but I couldn’t just step away. It was like if I broke it off, if I didn’t finish it right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.

And I know that sounds strange. The bay’s just the bay. But in that moment, it wasn’t like choosing what to have for lunch or whether to clean the rig. It felt… against me, kind of. Like I had to keep going.
