At the station I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose the order. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it doesn’t feel optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what they said it looked like. Repeating. I kept doing the same thing with the airway bags, moving them back into the pattern I knew was right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so it stayed in order.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, like, “Leave it alone, step away,” but I couldn’t just step away from it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nIt’s strange because at work you’re supposed to check things, you know? Equipment, bags, the truck, all of that. So part of me was thinking, well, I’m just making sure it’s right. But then I kept going back to it, and the numbers, and the bags… yeah, I guess that was repeating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that by repeating these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern, you're preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not like I had a clear plan in my head, like “if I move this bag, then that exact thing won’t happen.” It was more… if I didn’t keep the pattern right, the whole bay felt like it would go bad.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, I guess. I kept moving the airway bags back into the order I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, so I wouldn’t lose the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I heard him, but it felt wrong to break it.\n\nIt’s like when you’re checking equipment and something’s off, you don’t just leave it, you know? Except this wasn’t just checking anymore. It was the pattern. If I walked away before it was right, I felt like something bad was going to happen in the bay.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when you can't complete one of these patterns or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess. Fearful too, but it’s not like fear like a call coming in, or like when you know somebody’s really hurt. It’s more like this pressure, like everything is leaning the wrong way if I don’t finish it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept putting them back into the pattern that felt right, and saying the dispatch numbers out loud so I wouldn’t lose it. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away, and I heard him, but I couldn’t make myself do it. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nAnd the bay is supposed to be orderly anyway, you know, because if things aren’t where they’re supposed to be on a call, that can mess everything up. But this was… more than that. I knew he wanted me to stop, but stopping felt worse than getting yelled at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something very bad would happen if you didn't repeat these actions, like moving the airway bags into the right pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s what it felt like. Like if I didn’t put the airway bags back into the right pattern, something in the whole bay would go bad. Not even just the bags, it was the bay, the order of it.\n\nAnd I kept saying the dispatch numbers out loud, because if I lost them then the sequence was broken, and then… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain. At the station everything has to be ready, because one missing thing can mess up a call, and I’ve seen people get mad over a misplaced mask before, but this wasn’t just that.\n\nMy lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away from it, and I knew he was talking to me, but I couldn’t. It felt like walking away would make it worse somehow. Like I had to keep it right or the bay would go bad.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to put things in the right pattern or order, like with the airway bags, caused problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s caused problems. I mean, I’m not even really working the ambulance anymore. They tried putting me on desk duty, doing notes and equipment checks, which should’ve been easier, I guess, but even that got… messy. Unreliable. I’d get stuck on the order of things, or I’d have to go back over something, and then the notes wouldn’t get done right, or the checks would take too long.\n\nEventually they took me off the schedule. That was hard. I used to know where everything went in the rig, like exactly, and now it’s like I can’t even be trusted with the basic checks.\n\nAnd friends… I don’t really see people much now. I keep meaning to answer messages or go by someone’s place, but then the house is not right, or I get caught up trying to fix something, and then the whole day’s gone. I’m not keeping up with stuff at home like I used to either. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about patterns and sequences feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, or are they something you feel like you're generating yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I can separate that out right. It doesn’t feel like I sit down and decide, “Okay, make a pattern now.” It just kind of starts being there, like I notice the wrongness and then I’m already in it.\n\nBut “forcing its way in” sounds… I don’t know, like something kicking the door open. Sometimes it’s more like the room gets too loud, even if nobody’s talking. Like with supplies, you look at one bag and then suddenly you’re thinking about the whole shelf, and the shelf means the bay, and then you remember a call where something wasn’t where it was supposed to be— sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nI guess I’d say it feels like it happens to me more than I make it happen. But I don’t know if that’s the right answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you, like they're not really yours?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it alien exactly. It feels like… it’s mine because I’m the one doing it, you know? I was the one moving the airway bags around at the station, putting them in the pattern that felt right, saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nBut it also didn’t feel like normal me. Normal me would’ve heard my lieutenant say, “Step away,” and I would’ve stepped away. That day I couldn’t, because if I broke the sequence it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess it’s in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a part of me I chose. It feels like something I get stuck inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you want to have, or do you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t want them, no. I mean… maybe before, at work, being organized was a good thing. On the rig you need things where they belong, because if you’re reaching for an airway bag you don’t want to be guessing, you know?\n\nBut this isn’t that. They sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over. My lieutenant told me to stop, told me to step away, and I just… couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo yeah, I wish I could stop. I don’t want to be like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something you dislike having and wish you could control or eliminate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I dislike it. I don’t want it there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m proud of what happened at the station. I kept moving the airway bags around into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, and my lieutenant kept telling me to stop, to step away. I heard him, I did, but it was like if I stopped in the middle of it the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd that sounds stupid when I say it here, because airway bags are just airway bags, but at the time it didn’t feel small. It felt like the sequence mattered more than anything else. So yes, I wish I could control it. I wish I could just drop it when someone tells me to.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and behaviors about patterns and sequences made it difficult for you to interact with friends or family, or to enjoy activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. I don’t really see people much now.\n\nLike the guys used to call me for basketball, and I’d say I was coming, or at least I’d think about it. Now most days I just sit in my apartment. There’s the trays in the corner, food still sitting in the fridge, and I keep thinking I should cook something, because I used to like cooking, you know, doing it right, getting everything lined up on the counter—\n\nBut then I just don’t. I don’t call anybody back. I don’t start anything. It’s not even always the patterns right in that second… it’s like I can’t get myself to care enough to move. So yeah, friends, family, all that… I’ve kind of let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are inconsistent with your self-image or who you think you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t feel like me. I mean, I was always the guy who kept the rig straight, kept things where they belonged. That made sense. You check your airway bag, you know where the laryngoscope is, the mask, the tubing—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked exactly.\n\nBut what happened at the station… they sent me here because I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop and step away, and I just wouldn’t. I couldn’t. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad.\n\nThat’s not who I thought I was. I’m supposed to be useful there, not… standing in the bay doing that while people are telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about patterns and sequences are something that you are in control of, or do they feel like they are happening against your will?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I’m in control of it, no. I mean… I’m moving my hands, so I know it’s me doing it, but it feels like something’s pushing me along once the sequence starts.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags—I kept putting them into this pattern because it just felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I heard him, but I couldn’t just step away. It was like if I broke it off, if I didn’t finish it right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange. The bay’s just the bay. But in that moment, it wasn’t like choosing what to have for lunch or whether to clean the rig. It felt… against me, kind of. Like I had to keep going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… want to have them? No. I mean, who would want to be stuck on something like that?

But part of who I am… that’s harder, because I’ve always noticed how things line up. Even when I was a kid, I’d straighten stuff on the table, and my dad had this old toolbox in the garage where the sockets had to go smallest to biggest—well, not had to, but it made sense that way. And in EMS, you need order. If you don’t have order, people get hurt, you know?

But this isn’t like that exactly. Or maybe it started like that. I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking, “Yeah, this is me, this is what I want.” It just kind of takes over, and then afterwards I’m standing there like… why was I doing that? So I guess I don’t really know if it’s part of me or just something happening to me.
